# FOOTNOTES AND SCHOLARLY COMMENTARY



[^1]: King Augustus III of Poland (Frederick Augustus II of Saxony, reigned 1734–1763).

[^2]: Sylvester Lubieniecki-Rudnytskyi (Sylwester Rudnycki-Lubieniecki, c. 1708–1777), Basilian Bishop of Lutsk and Ostroh (1750–1777), Exarch of the Metropolitanate of Kyiv, Halych, and all Rus'.

[^3]: Hypatius Bilynskyi (Ipatij Bilyns'kyj, 1704–1771), Protoarchimandrite of the Basilian Order of Saint Basil the Great (OSBM, 1751–1771).

[^4]: The Byzantine Anno Mundi year 7269 corresponds to 1760/1761 AD (September 1 era).

[^5]: In this physical copy of the 1761 Pochaiv Menaion, Folio 3 (leaves p3–p4) was misbound immediately following the blank verso of the Title Page (leaf p2), preceding Folio 1 (leaf p5) and Folio 2 (leaves p7–p8). Leaf p3 opens with the conclusion of the second Old Testament Paremia for the feast of Saint Basil the Great (Proverbs 11:5–12 LXX), continuing directly from the catchword (*па́даетъ*) at the foot of Folio 2v (leaf p8).

[^6]: Proverbs 11:5–12 (LXX).

[^7]: Incipit of the idiomelon of the Circumcision, printed in full at Great Vespers on Folio 1v (leaf p6): *He Who descended, the Savior, unto the human race...* (*Сходя́й сп[а́]съ къ ро́ду человѣ́ческому...*).

[^8]: Psalm 48:4 [LXX 48:4; MT 49:3].

[^9]: Psalm 36:30 [LXX 36:30; MT 37:30].

[^10]: Here begins the true textual sequence of the Menaion for January, designated in the lower margin by gathering signature *A* (*а*). In the printed pagination, this leaf is Folio 1 recto.

[^11]: Slavonic: *по все́ послѣ́дованїе я́коже изда́ся на ряду́* (the entire liturgical sequence as set forth in the daily order of the Menaion).

[^12]: Genesis 17:1–7 (LXX).

[^13]: Genesis 17:7–14 (LXX).

[^14]: Proverbs 8:22–30 (LXX).

[^15]: Proverbs 10:31–11:5 (LXX).

[^16]: The text of the paremia breaks off here at the foot of Folio 2v (leaf p8) with the catchword *па́даетъ* (shall fall), and continues directly on Folio 3r (leaf p3): *па́даетъ непра́вда...* (see fol. 3r; physical leaf coordinate: leaf p3).

[^17]: Continued directly from the catchword (*брѣ́занъ*) at the foot of Folio 3v (leaf p4), concluding the Theotokion of the feast at Great Vespers: *therefore also circumcised was an eight-day-old man...*

[^18]: Saint Gregory of Nazianzus (the Theologian), *Oration 43: Funeral Oration on the Great Saint Basil, Bishop of Caesarea in Cappadocia* (PG 36:493–606). In the Byzantine and Ruthenian tradition, this lengthy homily was divided into four readings appointed across Matins.

[^19]: Psalm 48:4 [LXX 48:4; MT 49:3].

[^20]: Psalm 48:2 [LXX 48:2; MT 49:1].

[^21]: John 10:9–16 (Pericope 36: the Good Shepherd pericope appointed for holy hierarchs).

[^22]: Incipit of the idiomelon after Psalm 50: *Grace was poured out upon thy lips, O venerable father...* (*И́злїяся бл[а]года́ть на оустна́хъ твои́хъ...*), printed in full at the Litiya on Folio 3r (leaf p3).

[^23]: Prov. 1:7; 9:10: "The fear of the Lord is the beginning of wisdom."

[^24]: 1 Kings (1 Sam.) 2:10; 2:2: "The Lord shall give strength unto our kings, and shall exalt the horn of His anointed."

[^25]: Isa. 40:3; Matt. 3:3; LXX Ps. 28:3; 1 Cor. 1:24.

[^26]: Refers to St. Basil the Great's *Hexaemeron* (Nine Homilies on the Six Days of Creation), commenting upon the cosmogony revealed by God unto Moses on Mount Sinai.

[^27]: Cf. Gen. 41:48–56, where Joseph measured out and distributed grain to the hungry in Egypt; St. Basil is hymned as dispensing not merely perishable grain, but the Divine Bread of the Holy Eucharist in the Liturgy.

[^28]: Phil. 4:7: "And the peace of God, which surpasses all understanding, shall keep your hearts and minds through Christ Jesus."

[^29]: Jonah 2:3, 7.

[^30]: LXX Ps. 77:25; Wisdom 16:20: "bread of angels."

[^31]: Isa. 40:3; Mark 1:3; John 1:23.

[^33]: Dan. 3:1–23.

[^34]: The theological and ascetic friendship of St. Basil the Great and St. Gregory the Theologian (Nazianzen), commemorated in Gregory's Funeral Oration (*Oratio 43.20*): "We seemed to have two bodies, but a single soul."

[^35]: Exod. 3:2: the burning bush on Horeb/Sinai prefiguring the virginal conception and the Incarnation of God the Word.

[^36]: Dan. 3:49–50 (LXX / Prayer of Azariah and the Song of the Three Holy Children).

[^37]: LXX Ps. 103:3: "Who covers His upper chambers with waters: Who makes the clouds His chariot: Who walks upon the wings of the winds."

[^41]: LXX Ps. 48:4: "My mouth shall speak wisdom, and the meditation of my heart understanding."

[^42]: Col. 2:8–12: pericope 254 (Feast of the Circumcision).

[^43]: Heb. 7:26–8:2: pericope 318 (Hierarch St. Basil the Great).

[^44]: LXX Ps. 79:2: "Give ear, O Shepherd of Israel, Thou that leadest Joseph like a flock."

[^45]: LXX Ps. 36:30: "The mouth of the righteous shall meditate wisdom, and his tongue shall speak of judgment."

[^46]: Luke 2:20–21, 40–52: pericope 6 (Feast of the Circumcision of the Lord).

[^47]: John 10:9–16 (pericope 104 of the Slavonic Gospel lectionary).

[^48]: The megalynarion of Saint Basil the Great, *All of creation rejoices in thee* (*Ѡ҆ тебѣ̀ ра́дꙋетсѧ*), sung in place of *It is truly meet* at the Divine Liturgy of Saint Basil.

[^49]: Ps. 148:1 (LXX).

[^50]: Ps. 111:6 (LXX).

[^51]: Detailed rubrics for the concurrence of the Feast of the Circumcision and Saint Basil the Great with a Sunday (the Sunday before Theophany).

[^52]: Saint Gregory the Theologian, *Oration 43: Funeral Oration on Saint Basil the Great* (*In laudem Basilii Magni*).

[^53]: The dual katavasiai of Theophany: the first canon by Saint Cosmas the Melodist (*Глꙋбины̀ ѿкры́лъ є҆́сть дно̀* — "The Lord mighty in battles uncovered the bottom of the deep") and the second (iambic) canon by Saint John of Damascus (*Ше́ствꙋетъ мо́рскꙋю* — "He marches over the surging deep").

[^653]: Third Sticheron at the Aposticha, with Psalm 76:17 (LXX) psalm-verse.

[^654]: Doxasticon at the Aposticha (*Glory... Both now...*), Tone 6, Anatolian composition (*А҆нато́лїево* / *Ἀνατολικόν*), alluding to Luke 1:41 and Psalm 113:3 (LXX).

[^655]: Rubric for the conclusion of Matins on January 5.

[^656]: Codicological Typikon rubric governing the fasting and scheduling dispensation for the Royal Hours on the Eve of Theophany (Paramon / *Навече́рїе*) according to the day of the week on which the Feast falls, specifying dietary rules for monastics and laity (*штѝ съ со́комъ, да кꙋтїю*).

[^657]: Service headpiece and title for the Royal Hours sung on the Eve of the Holy Theophany (*Послѣдованіе часовъ пѣва́емыхъ въ навечеріи просвѣще́нія*), traditionally designated as the Great or Imperial Hours (*Царскіе часы́*).

[^658]: Rubric prescribing the bell summons (*зна́менуетъ къ кампа́нъ*) at the beginning of the second hour of the day (approximately 8:00 AM) and liturgical vestment protocol (priest in phelonion, deacon in sticharion).

[^659]: Ceremonial rubric governing the solemn translation of the Holy Gospel Book through the Holy Doors to the adorned analogion in the nave or refectory, followed by the initial blessing and full censing.

[^660]: Psalm 5 (*Глаго́лы моѧ̑ внꙋшѝ гдⷭ҇и*), the opening psalm of the First Royal Hour, invoking morning prayer and divine guidance against deceitful adversaries (LXX Ps. 5).

[^661]: Conclusion of Psalm 22 (*Гдⷭ҇ь пасе́тъ мѧ̀*, LXX Ps. 22), the prophetic pastoral psalm expressing trust in the divine Shepherd Who prepares a mystical table and anoints with oil.

[^662]: Psalm 26 (*Гдⷭ҇ь просвѣще́нїе моѐ*, LXX Ps. 26), third psalm of the First Royal Hour, proclaiming the Lord as illumination and defense (*просвѣще́нїе*) before the baptismal mystery.

[^663]: Troparion of the Forefeast of Theophany, Tone 4 (*Возвраща́шесѧ, и҆ногда̀ і҆орда́нъ рѣка̀*), typologically linking Elijah's cloak dividing the Jordan to Christ entering the waters.

[^664]: Conclusion of the Forefeast Troparion (*и҆ бы́сть є҆мꙋ̀ сꙋ́хъ пꙋ́ть*), meditating on the passage of Jordan as a prefigurement of holy baptism.

[^665]: Theotokion of the First Hour (*Что́ тѧ нарече́мъ, ѽ бл҃годатнаѧ*), addressing the Mother of God with successive Scriptural epithets (Heaven, Paradise, Virgin, Pure Mother).

[^666]: First Royal Hour Sticheron/Troparion, Tone 8, by Sophronius, Patriarch of Jerusalem (*Дне́сь вѡ́дъ ѡ҆сщ҃а́етсѧ є҆стество̀*), commemorating the cosmic sanctification of the waters at Christ's baptism.

[^667]: Second Sticheron of the First Royal Hour, Tone 8 (*Ꙗ҆́кѡ чл҃вѣ́къ на рѣкꙋ̀ прише́лъ є҆сѝ Хрⷭ҇тѐ цр҃ю̀*), with its appointed festal refrain from LXX Ps. 41:7 (*Сегѡ̀ ра́ди помѧнꙋ́хъ тѧ̀*).

[^668]: Sticheron at *Glory... Both now...*, Tone 8 (*Ко гла́сꙋ вопїю́щагѡ въ пꙋсты́ни*), portraying the dialogue of John the Baptist trembling before the naked Creator requesting baptism.

[^669]: Prokeimenon of the First Royal Hour, Tone 4 (LXX Ps. 17:14, 2-3: *И҆ возгремѣ̀ съ нб҃сѐ гдⷭ҇ь*).

[^670]: Old Testament Paremia from Isaiah 35:1-10 (*Та́кѡ глаго́летъ гдⷭ҇ь: да возвесели́тсѧ пꙋсты́нѧ жꙗ́ждꙋщаѧ*), prophesying the blooming of the desert, the healing of the weak, and the joy of the redeemed.

[^671]: Conclusion of the First Hour Old Testament Reading (Isaiah 35:3-10), depicting the joyous return of the redeemed on the holy way.

[^672]: Epistle reading of the First Royal Hour from Acts of the Apostles (Pericope 33, Acts 13:25-33), quoting St. John the Baptist's testimony that he is unworthy to loose Christ's sandals, followed by Paul's preaching of the resurrection in Antioch of Pisidia.

[^673]: Gospel reading of the First Royal Hour from Matthew 3:1-11, recounting John the Baptist's ministry in the Jordan wilderness, warning the Pharisees and Sadducees, and prophesying Christ's baptism with the Holy Spirit and with fire.

[^674]: Verses from Psalm 118:133-135 appointed after the Gospel at the First Hour (*Стѡпы̀ моѧ̑ напра́ви*).

[^675]: Verse from Psalm 70:8 (*Да и҆спо́лнѧтсѧ ѹ҆ста̀ моѧ̑ хвале́нїѧ твоегѡ̀ гдⷭ҇и*), sung after the psalm verses.

[^676]: Kontakion of the Forefeast of Theophany, Tone 4 (*Во стрꙋ́ѧхъ дне́сь і҆орда́нскихъ бы́въ гдⷭ҇ь*).

[^677]: General Prayer of the Hours (*И҆́же на всѧ́кое вре́мѧ, и҆ на всѧ́кїй ча́съ*), attributed to St. Basil the Great.

[^678]: Concluding Prayer of the First Hour (*Хрⷭ҇тѐ свѣ́те и҆́стинный*), invoking Christ the true Light to illuminate every man coming into the world.

[^679]: Liturgical headpiece and opening formula for the Third Royal Hour (*И҆ ЧА́СЪ ТРЕ́ТЇИ*), commemorating the descent of the Holy Spirit and the baptismal mystery.

[^680]: Psalm 28 (*Принеси́те гдⷭ҇ви сы́нове бж҃їи*, LXX Ps. 28), prophetic psalm of the voice of the Lord upon the waters (*Гла́съ гдⷭ҇нь на вода́хъ*).

[^681]: Conclusion of Psalm 41 (LXX Ps. 41:3-12: *къ бг҃ꙋ крѣ́пкомꙋ живомꙋ̀*), the prophetic psalm of spiritual thirst and tears, containing the festal refrain: "Therefore will I remember Thee from the land of Jordan and Hermon" (*Сегѡ̀ ра́ди помѧнꙋ́хъ тѧ̀ ѿ землѝ і҆орда́нскїа*).

[^682]: Psalm 50 (*Поми́лꙋй мѧ̀ бж҃е*, LXX Ps. 50), the great penitential psalm of David, appointed at the Third Royal Hour.

[^683]: Conclusion of Psalm 50 (*чественнымъ ѹ҆тверди́ мѧ*, LXX Ps. 50:14-21), petitioning for the cleansing of heart, renewal of the right spirit, and the spiritual sacrifice acceptable to God.

[^684]: Theotokion of the Third Hour (*Бц҃е ты̀ є҆сѝ лоза̀ и҆́стиннаѧ*), entreating the Mother of God as the true Vine that budded the Fruit of life.

[^685]: First Sticheron of the Third Royal Hour, Tone 8 (*Предте́чи и҆ крⷭ҇ти́телѧ и҆ проро́ка*), depicting the trembling right hand of John the Baptist as he beholds the Lamb of God and cries: "I dare not touch Thy head, O Word!"

[^686]: Second Sticheron of the Third Royal Hour, Tone 4 (*Трⷪ҇ца бг҃ъ на́шъ, себѐ на́мъ дне́сь нераздѣ́лнѡ ꙗ҆вѝ*), confessing the undivided revelation of the Holy Trinity at Christ's baptism: the Father witnessing, the Spirit descending in the form of a dove, and the Son bowing His head.

[^687]: Sticheron at *Glory...*, Tone 5 (*Грѧды́й со пло́тїю ко і҆орда́нꙋ гдⷭ҇и*), contemplating Christ coming in the flesh to deliver human nature from the snares of the serpent.

[^688]: Conclusion of the Third Royal Hour Sticheron at *Glory...*, Tone 5 (*прельсти́вшихсѧ на́съ ꙗ҆́кѡ бл҃гоꙋтро́бенъ*), asking that the Holy Spirit Who rested upon Christ in Jordan may dwell within the faithful.

[^689]: Prokeimenon of the Third Royal Hour, Tone 6 (LXX Ps. 76:17, 2: *Ви́дѣша тѧ̀ вѡ́ды бж҃е*).

[^690]: Old Testament Paremia of the Third Royal Hour from Isaiah 1:16-20 (*Та́кѡ глаго́летъ гдⷭ҇ь: и҆змы́йтесѧ, и҆ чи́сти бꙋ́дете*), exhorting purification from sin and promising cleansing whiter than snow or wool.

[^691]: Epistle reading of the Third Royal Hour from Acts of the Apostles (Pericope 42, Acts 19:1-8), recounting St. Paul rebaptizing disciples of John at Ephesus in the name of the Lord Jesus and imparting the Holy Spirit.

[^692]: Gospel reading of the Third Royal Hour from Mark 1:1-8, the opening of Mark's Gospel detailing John's baptism of repentance and prophecy of the Spirit-Baptizer.

[^693]: Verses appointed after the Gospel at the Third Hour from Psalm 67:20-21 (*Гдⷭ҇ь бг҃ъ бл҃гослове́нъ*).

[^694]: Concluding Prayer of the Third Hour by St. Mardarios (*Влⷣко бж҃е ѻ҆́ч҃е вседержи́телю*), invoking the Holy Trinity with profound contrition.

[^695]: Rubric specifying that at the Third and Sixth Hours the deacon performs a lesser censing restricted to the Holy Gospel, local icons, the superior, and the choir ranks (*кади́тъ дїа́конъ є҆ѵ́лїе и҆ і҆кѡ́ны, и҆ настоя́телѧ, и҆ ли́ки то́кмѡ*), rather than censing the entire temple.

[^696]: Liturgical headpiece and opening formula for the Sixth Royal Hour (*ЧА́СЪ ШЕ́СТЫЙ*), commemorating the crucifixion of Christ and the noon-day light of His manifestation.

[^697]: Opening of Psalm 73 (*Вскꙋ́ю бж҃е ѿри́нꙋлъ є҆сѝ до конца̀?*, LXX Ps. 73), lamenting the destruction of the sanctuary and pleading for God's ancient covenant remembrance.

[^698]: Continuation of Psalm 73:3-4 (LXX). Note that the Pochaiv 1761 edition's printed foliation jumps directly from Fol. 49 (*м҃ѳ*) on physical leaf p99/p100 to Fol. 51 (*н҃а*) on physical leaf p101, skipping numeral 50 (*н*), but the text flows seamlessly without any omission or textual gap across the bottom custos *рꙋ́цѣ*.

[^699]: Conclusion of Psalm 73:5-23 (LXX), the second psalm of the Sixth Royal Hour, prophetically prefiguring Christ's crushing of the dragons' heads in the waters of Jordan (*Ты̀ сте́рлъ є҆сѝ главы̑ смїє́въ въ водѣ̀*).

[^700]: Opening verses of Psalm 76:2-7 (LXX: *Гла́сомъ мои́мъ ко гдⷭ҇ꙋ воззва́хъ*), the third psalm of the Sixth Royal Hour, expressing deep soul-anguish and earnest nocturnal seeking of God.

[^701]: Conclusion of Psalm 76:8-21 (LXX), culminating in the cosmic witness of the waters (*Ви́дѣша тѧ̀ во́ды бж҃е*), the standard Theophany psalmody refrains.

[^702]: Psalm 90:1-16 (LXX: *Живы́й въ по́мощи вы́шнѧгѡ*), appointed at the Sixth Hour, celebrating divine protection against demonic snares and trampling the serpent.

[^703]: Festal Troparion of the Forefeast of Theophany, Tone 4 (*Возвраща́шеся и҆ногда̀ і҆орда́нъ рѣка̀*), recalling Elisha dividing the Jordan with Elijah's mantle (4 Kgdms / 2 Kings 2:14) as a type of holy baptism.

[^704]: Standard Theotokion of the Sixth Hour (*Ꙗ҆́кѡ не и҆́мамы дерзнове́нїя*), appealing to the maternal boldness of the Virgin Mother before her Son.

[^705]: First Sticheron of the Sixth Royal Hour, Tone 8 (*Та́кѡ глаго́летъ гдⷭ҇ь ко і҆ѡа́ннꙋ*), embodying Christ's command to John to touch His divine head and drown the demonic adversary hiding in the waters.

[^706]: Second Sticheron of the Sixth Royal Hour, Tone 2 (*Дне́сь ѱало́мское прⷪ҇ро́чество*), applying Psalm 113:3 to the baptism of Christ and the cleansing of the nations from idolatry.

[^707]: Opening of the Sticheron at *Glory...*, Tone 5 (*Что̀ возвраща́еши твоѧ̑ во́ды, ѽ і҆орда́не?*), portraying dramatic personified dialogue between the prophet/church and the river Jordan trembling before the divine fire.

[^708]: Conclusion of the Sixth Royal Hour Sticheron at *Glory...*, Tone 5 (*і҆ѡа́ннъ свидѣ́тельствꙋетъ мѝ*), quoting John's testimony of the Lamb of God (John 1:29) and invoking Christ's theophanic manifestation for human salvation.

[^709]: Prokeimenon of the Sixth Royal Hour, Tone 4 (LXX Ps. 76:20, 19: *Въ мо́ри пꙋтїѐ твоѝ*), witnessing the path of God through the depths of the sea and the voice of thunder.

[^710]: Old Testament Paremia of the Sixth Royal Hour from Isaiah 12:3-6 (*Почерпи́те во́дꙋ съ весе́лїемъ*), foretelling joyful drawing of living water from the springs of salvation.

[^711]: Epistle reading of the Sixth Royal Hour from Romans 6:3-11 (Pericope 91), the classical Pauline baptismal theology of dying and rising with Christ into newness of life.

[^712]: Gospel reading of the Sixth Royal Hour from Mark 1:9-15 (Pericope 2), detailing the Baptism of Jesus, the heavenly voice, the forty days' temptation in the wilderness, and the proclamation of the Kingdom.

[^713]: Verses appointed after the Gospel at the Sixth Hour from Psalm 78:8-9 (*Ско́рѡ да предварѧ́тъ ны̀ щедрѡ́ты твоѧ̑ гдⷭ҇и*), pleading for rapid divine deliverance.

[^714]: Concluding Prayer of the Sixth Hour by St. Basil the Great (*Бж҃е и҆ гдⷭ҇и си́лъ*), praising Christ's destruction of the handwriting of sins on the Cross and petitioning for spiritual illumination.

[^715]: Liturgical headpiece and opening for the Ninth Royal Hour (*ЧА́СЪ ДЕВЯ́ТЫЙ*), the final hour of the Theophany eve vigil services, commemorating Christ's death and baptismal consecration.

[^716]: Opening of Psalm 92:1-2 (LXX: *Гдⷭ҇ь воцр҃и́ся, въ лѣ́потꙋ ѡ҆блече́ся*), proclaiming the Lord's eternal kingdom and mastery over the cosmic floods.

[^717]: Conclusion of Psalm 92:2-5 (LXX), exalting the voice of many waters and the holiness of the house of God.

[^718]: Psalm 113:1-26 (LXX: *Во и҆схо́дѣ і҆и҃левѣ ѿ є҆гѵ́пта*), the great festal Theophany psalm witnessing Jordan turning back and the futility of pagan idols.

[^719]: Conclusion of Psalm 113 (LXX), confessing the living God of heaven Who blesses all who fear Him.

[^720]: Opening of Psalm 85:1-2 (LXX: *Приклонѝ гдⷭ҇и ᲂу҆́хо твоѐ*), the prayer of David expressing deep humility and supplication for divine preservation.

[^721]: Conclusion of Psalm 85:3-17 (LXX), completing the third psalm of the Ninth Royal Hour with petitions for divine comfort, strength, and a token for good.

[^722]: Forefeast Troparion of Theophany, Tone 4 (*Возвраща́шеся и҆ногда̀ і҆орда́нъ рѣка̀*), repeated at each of the Royal Hours.

[^723]: Standard Theotokion of the Ninth Hour (*И҆́же на́съ ра́ди рожде́йсѧ ѿ дв҃ы*), pleading Christ's cross, death-trampling Resurrection, and the Mother of God's intercession for a despairing people.

[^724]: First Sticheron of the Ninth Royal Hour, Tone 7 (*Оу҆́жасъ бѣ̀ ви́дѣти*), beholding Christ stripped in Jordan to be baptized by a servant.

[^725]: Second Sticheron of the Ninth Royal Hour, Tone 2 (*Є҆гда̀ къ себѣ̀ грѧдꙋ́ща предте́ча*), proclaiming Christ turning slaves into sons of God through the water of divine baptism.

[^726]: Sticheron at *Glory... Both now...*, Tone 5 (*Рꙋ́кꙋ твою̀ прикосновшꙋюся пречи́стомꙋ ве́рхꙋ влⷣчню*), an impassioned invocation of the Baptist, beseeching him to lift his sanctified hand and dove-beholding eyes in intercession for the gathered flock.

[^727]: Rubric directing that at this fourth station during the Ninth Hour (*На д҃ мѣ́стѣ*), the deacon performs a full censing of the entire temple (*кади́тъ дїа́конъ ве́сь хра́мъ*), mirroring the solemn conclusion of the Royal Hours.

[^728]: Prokeimenon of the Ninth Royal Hour, Tone 3 (LXX Ps. 26:1: *Гдⷭ҇ь просвѣще́нїе моѐ*).

[^729]: Conclusion of the Ninth Royal Hour Paremia from Isaiah 49:13-15, proclaiming God's tender and unfailing maternal love toward Zion, promising never to forget her.

[^730]: Epistle reading of the Ninth Royal Hour from Titus 2:11-14 and 3:4-7 (Pericope 302), foundational text for the theological identity between Theophany and the washing of regeneration (*ба́нею пакибытїя̀*).

[^731]: Gospel reading of the Ninth Royal Hour from Matthew 3:13-17 (Pericope 6), recording Christ's baptism by John in the Jordan, the descent of the Spirit as a dove, and the Father's voice.

[^732]: Verses appointed after the Gospel at the Ninth Hour from the Prayer of Azariah (LXX Daniel 3:34-35 / Song of the Three Holy Children 11-12: *Не преда́ждь на́съ до конца̀*).

[^733]: Concluding Prayer of the Ninth Hour by St. Basil the Great (*Влⷣко гдⷭ҇и і҆и҃се хрⷭ҇тѐ бж҃е на́шъ, долготерпѣ́вый*), reflecting upon Christ's ninth-hour death on the Cross and admission of the Good Thief into Paradise.

[^734]: Opening of Psalm 102:1-5 (LXX: *Бл҃гословѝ дꙋшѐ моѧ̀ гдⷭ҇а*), introducing the Service of the Typika (*Изобрази́тельныѧ*) appended directly to the Royal Hours on the Eve of Theophany.

[^735]: Foliation marker: Fol. 56r (н̃ѕ). The text concludes Psalm 102 (LXX 102:6–22) within the Typika (Obednitsa) office for the Eve of Theophany.

[^736]: Psalm 145 (LXX 145:1–10), the second antiphon of the Typika, followed by the hymn of the Incarnation attributed to Emperor Justinian, Only-begotten Son (Ὁ Μονογενὴς Υἱός).

[^737]: Foliation marker: Fol. 56v (unnumbered verso). Conclusion of the antiphon hymn Only-begotten Son and Word of God.

[^738]: The Beatitudes (Matthew 5:3–12) recited recto tono (*без̾ пѣ́нїѧ*) as customary during Lenten and Fast Eve Typika when the Divine Liturgy is deferred to Vespers.

[^739]: Rubric and opening articles of the Nicene Creed as articulated at the First Ecumenical Council (Nicaea, 325), designated in Ruthenian Typika for recitation during the non-Eucharistic Typika office.

[^740]: Foliation marker: Fol. 57r (н̃з). Heading denoting the pneumatological and ecclesiological articles established at the Second Ecumenical Council (Constantinople, 381).

[^741]: Ruthenian Uniate recension witness: Church Slavonic *и҆́же ѿ ѻ҆ц҃а̀ и҆ сн҃а и҆сходѧ́щаго* ("Who proceeds from the Father and the Son"). This reflects the formal inclusion of the *Filioque* in Ukrainian Greek Catholic liturgical editions printed under the authority of the Synod of Zamość (1720) at the Basilian press of the Pochaiv Lavra.

[^742]: The classical penitential prayer *Ѡсла́би, ѡ҆ста́ви* (Remitte, dimitte), recited during the Hours, Typika, and Compline.

[^743]: Kontakion of the Forefeast of Theophany, Tone 4: *Во струѧ́хъ дне́сь і҆орда́нскихъ бы́въ гдⷭь*.

[^744]: Prayer of the Holy Trinity (*Пресвѧта́ѧ трⷪце, є҆диносꙋ́щнаѧ*), concluding with the Eucharistic acclamation of Philippians 2:11.

[^745]: Foliation marker: Fol. 57v (unnumbered verso). Conclusion of Psalm 33 (LXX 33:9–23) recited during the Typika.

[^746]: Rubrical regulation regarding the occurrence of the Eve of Theophany on Saturday or Sunday: the strict Paramon fast is mitigated, the Royal Hours are transferred to Friday, and cheese and eggs are permitted on Friday according to the Ruthenian Typikon.

[^747]: Post-liturgical fast mitigation and dinner rules for the Eve of Theophany, distinguishing the light collation of bread and wine after Liturgy from the full evening meal with olive oil after Vespers.

[^748]: Foliation marker: Fol. 58r (н̃и). The primary headpiece for the Great Feast of the Holy Theophany (January 6), preceded by a woodcut engraving depicting the Baptism of Christ in the Jordan by John the Baptist.

[^749]: Opening rubrics for Great Vespers conjoined with the Divine Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great, followed by the Idiomelon stichera attributed to Saint John the Monk (St. John Damascene).

[^750]: Foliation marker: Fol. 58v (unnumbered verso). The conclusion of the Stichera on "Lord, I have cried," celebrating Christ crushing the heads of the noetic dragons in Jordan (cf. Ps. 73:13).

[^751]: The first Old Testament reading (Paroimia) of the Theophany Vigil: Genesis 1:1–13 (the First Three Days of Creation).

[^752]: The second Old Testament reading: Exodus 14:15–18, 21–23, 27–29 (the Miraculous Crossing of the Red Sea).

[^753]: Foliation marker: Fol. 59r (н̃ѳ). The conclusion of the second reading from Exodus 14.

[^754]: The third Old Testament reading: Exodus 15:22–27; 16:1 (the Bitter Waters of Marah Sweetened by the Wood, a prefiguration of the Cross, and the arrival at Elim with its twelve springs and seventy palm trees).

[^755]: The solemn Troparion chanted after the third reading with verses of Psalm 66 (LXX 66:2–8), sung antiphonally by both choirs before the Royal Doors with the refrain *Да просвѣти́ши во тмѣ̀ сѣдѧ́щыѧ*.

[^756]: Foliation marker: Fol. 59v (unnumbered verso). Verses 2 and 3 of Psalm 66 and the doxological conclusion of the Troparion in Tone 5.

[^757]: The fourth Old Testament reading: Joshua 3:7–8, 14–17 (the Miraculous Crossing of the Jordan with the Ark of the Covenant, halting the waters).

[^758]: The fifth Old Testament reading: 4 Kings 2:6–14 (the Fiery Ascension of Elijah and the Parting of the Jordan by Elisha with Elijah's Mantle).

[^759]: Foliation marker: Fol. 60r (ѯ). Conclusion of 4 Kings 2.

[^760]: Church Slavonic *а҆пфѡ́* transliterates the Septuagint Greek particle *ἀφφώ* (2 Kings / 4 Kings 2:14: ποῦ ἐστιν ὁ θεὸς Ἠλιοὺ ἀφφώ;), rendering the Hebrew אַף־הוּא (*ʾap-huʾ*, "even He!").

[^761]: The sixth Old Testament reading: 4 Kings 5:9–14 (the Cleansing of the Leprous Syrian Captain Naaman in the Waters of the Jordan).

[^762]: The second Vigil Troparion chanted after the sixth reading in Tone 6, with its festive refrain *Гдѣ̀ бо и҆мѣ́лъ бы свѣ́тъ тво́й возсїѧ́ти* ("For where should Thy light have shone forth...").

[^763]: Foliation marker: Fol. 60v (unnumbered verso). Continuation of the Vigil Troparion of Tone 6 with the verses of Psalm 92 (LXX 92:1–5: *The Lord is King*).

[^764]: The seventh Old Testament reading: Isaiah 1:16–20 (the Call to Purification, Repentance, and Moral Renewal).

[^765]: The eighth Old Testament reading: Genesis 32:1–5 (Jacob Meeting the Angelic Host of God at Mahanaim).

[^782]: Liturgical commemoration formula of the Ruthenian Uniate episcopate in the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth.

[^783]: Liturgical petition for the ruling monarch of the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth (King Augustus III of Poland, Grand Duke of Lithuania, r. 1734–1763).

[^784]: Codicological rubric regarding the regional liturgical variation between the Constantinopolitan and Athonite recensions and the Ruthenian and Slavic practice of reciting this introductory prayer of Saint Sophronius.

[^785]: Attributed to Saint Sophronius I, Patriarch of Jerusalem (ca. 560–638).

[^786]: This elaborate hymnographic invocation (*Днесь...* / *Hodie...*) forms the ancient festal encomium attributed to Saint Sophronius of Jerusalem, parallel to the *Praeconium Paschale* (*Exsultet*) of the Western rite.

[^787]: The tri-candle immersion ritual (*трисвѣщникъ* / *тройца*) during the Great Blessing of Waters on Theophany Eve and Theophany Day.

[^788]: Psalm 103:2 (LXX) / Psalm 104:2 (MT): "Who stretchest out the heaven like a curtain."

[^789]: Ineffable, unoriginate, and uncircumscribed: Church Slavonic *неѡписанный, безнача́лный же и҆ неизгла́голанный* (literally *uncircumscribed/undescribed, without beginning, and ineffable/unutterable*).

[^790]: Crush the heads of the dragons: Cf. Psalm 73:13 (LXX) [Ps. 74:13 MT]: *Thou didst break the heads of the dragons in the water.*

[^791]: Cleave the rock: Cf. Psalm 77:15, 20 (LXX) [Ps. 78:15, 20 MT].

[^792]: Turn Israel through Elijah from the error of Baal: Cf. 3 Kings (1 Kings MT) 18:36–39.

[^793]: Commemoration of the Pope of Rome and the King: Church Slavonic *па́пꙋ ри́мскаго, и҆ раба̀ твоего̀ бл҃говѣ́рнаго короля̀ на́шего*. The 1761 Pochaiv Menaion, published under the auspices of the Ukrainian Greek Catholic Church (Order of Saint Basil the Great) during the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth, prescribes the liturgical commemoration of the Pope of Rome (Clement XIII in 1761) and the Polish King (Augustus III of Poland, 1733–1763).

[^794]: With basil: Church Slavonic *васі́лкомъ* (instrumental of *васи́локъ*, sweet basil), traditionally bundled as an aspergillum for the blessing and sprinkling of waters.

[^795]: Chrysostom citation: St. John Chrysostom, *Homily on the Baptism of Christ* (PG 49:363–372).

[^796]: St. John Chrysostom incipit: *Omnes vos hodie in laetitia estis, ego vero solus in moerore prae aegritudine*, opening line of the homily *De baptismo Christi* (PG 49:363).

[^797]: Saturday/Sunday Eve Fasting Rule: If the Paramon (Eve) of Theophany falls on a Saturday or Sunday, strict fasting is relaxed to wine and oil, and the strict fast is observed on the preceding Friday.

[^798]: Heirmos of Ode 9: The festal megalynarion (*Zadostoinik*) replacing *It is truly meet* (*Dostoyno yest*) at the Divine Liturgy.

[^1928]: Christ as the messianic "rod of strength" sent forth from Zion (Ps. 109:2 [110:2] LXX) raising fallen Adam from hades.

[^1929]: Supplication for the maternal intercession of the Virgin before the throne of her Son and Master.

[^1930]: Conclusion of the Heirmos of Ode 9 (Saint's Canon), the Virgin's womb revealed as more spacious than the heavens (*Platytera ton ouranon*).

[^1931]: Anthony celebrated as the primordial rule (*kanon*) and lawgiver of monastic asceticism.

[^1932]: Intercessory petition to Anthony for the remission of sins and eternal rejoicing for his venerators.

[^1933]: Anthony adorned with the triple crown of prophet, martyr, and supreme leader of monastics.

[^1934]: Marian Theotokion confessing virginal conception without seed and parturition without pain or corruption.

[^1935]: Katavasia of Ode 9 (Meeting of the Lord, Tone 3), fulfilling the Mosaic law of the dedication of the firstborn (Ex. 13:2; Lk. 2:23).

[^1936]: Exapostilarion of St. Anthony, Tone 3 (*O Changeless Light*), Anthony dispersing demons with lightning fire.

[^1937]: Exapostilarion Theotokion, the Virgin bearing the supersubstantial Word in her arms.

[^1938]: Conclusion of the first Sticheron at the Praises for St. Anthony (Tone 8, *O most marvelous wonder*).

[^1939]: Praises Sticheron 2, honoring Anthony's path through the untrodden desert as monastic guide.

[^1940]: Praises Sticheron 3, recalling Anthony shutting himself in a tomb and overcoming physical assaults of demons through prayer (cf. *Vita Antonii*, 8-10).

[^1941]: Doxasticon at the Praises (Tone 8), Anthony ascending to the heavenly Jerusalem via the chariot of virtues.

[^1942]: Prokeimenon at the Liturgy for venerable monastics (Ps. 115:6, 3 [116:15, 12] LXX).

[^1943]: Alleluia at the Liturgy (Ps. 111:1-2 [112:1-2] LXX).

[^1944]: Communion Verse (Communion Hymn) for the righteous (Ps. 111:6b [112:6b] LXX).

[^1945]: Commemoration of the great Alexandrian hierarchs and defenders of Nicene and Ephesian Orthodoxy, SS. Athanasius the Great (c. 296–373) and Cyril of Alexandria (c. 376–444).

[^1946]: Sticheron 1 of St. Athanasius at "Lord, I have cried" (Tone 4, *As one valiant among martyrs*), extolling his five exiles and championing of the *Homoousios* against Arianism.

[^1947]: Sticheron 2 of St. Athanasius, venerating him as the immovable pillar of the Church against heretical deceit.

[^1948]: Sticheron 3 of St. Athanasius, describing his spiritual discipline and pastoral charism received through the anointing of the Holy Spirit.

[^1949]: Sticheron 4 (Cyril 1), portraying St. Cyril driving away the noetic wolves (Nestorianism) from Christ's flock.

[^1950]: Sticheron 5 (Cyril 2), celebrating Cyril's defense of the Theotokos and the Incarnation of the Word by the illumination of the Holy Spirit.

[^1951]: Conclusion of Sticheron 6 at "Lord, I have cried," honoring St. Cyril of Alexandria as the foremost champion of the Council of Ephesus (431 AD) and defender of the Holy Trinity.

[^1952]: Doxasticon at "Lord, I have cried" (Tone 6, composed by Patriarch Germanus of Constantinople), praising Athanasius for upholding the Nicene definition of the consubstantial Trinity against Arius.

[^1953]: Stavrotheotokion at "Lord, I have cried" (Tone 6, *On the third day*), the Virgin's lamentation before her crucified Son.

[^1954]: Aposticha Doxasticon (Tone 3, by Patriarch Germanus), allegorizing Athanasius as the spiritual Nile whose streams of theology fertilize the Church and whose name signifies immortality (*athanasia*).

[^1955]: Aposticha Stavrotheotokion (Tone 3), the Virgin beholding the fulfillment of Simeon's prophecy (Lk. 2:35) at the Cross.

[^1956]: General Troparion (Troparion) for Hierarchs (Tone 4), supplicating divine mercy through their holy intercession.

[^1957]: Festal Troparion of SS. Athanasius and Cyril (Tone 3), celebrating them as triumphant trophy-bearers of Orthodoxy who enriched the Church with true dogma.

[^1958]: Heirmos of Ode 1 (Canon of St. Athanasius, Tone 8), Moses' rod dividing the Red Sea and submerging Pharaoh (Ex. 14:26-28).

[^1959]: Opening Troparion of Ode 1, quoting the opening of St. Gregory the Theologian's *Panegyric on Athanasius* (*Oration 21*): to praise Athanasius is to praise virtue itself.

[^1960]: Troparion of St. Athanasius, beseeching the saint to accept the hymnographer's insufficient praises.

[^1961]: Troparion petitioning the forgiveness of the saint and the granting of the Holy Spirit's grace.

[^1962]: Troparion extolling Athanasius' early wisdom and mastery over youth's passions (Wis. 4:8-9).

[^1963]: Theotokion of Ode 1 (Athanasius' Canon), quoting Ps. 86:3 [87:3] LXX: "Glorious things are spoken of thee, O city of God."

[^1964]: Heirmos of Ode 1 (Canon of St. Cyril, Tone 4), Israel crossing the Red Sea and Moses' cruciform posture defeating Amalek (Ex. 17:11-12).

[^1965]: Opening Troparion of Ode 1 (Canon of St. Cyril), invoking Cyril as a radiant luminary illuminating the Church.

[^1966]: Troparion venerating Cyril as a keeper of Christ's commandments and living temple of the All-Holy Trinity.

[^1967]: Troparion praising Cyril's defense of Orthodox Christology against Nestorianism and related heresies.

[^1968]: Theotokion of Ode 1 (Cyril's Canon), confessing the Incarnation of the Second Person of the Trinity through the good will of the Father and the cooperation of the Holy Spirit.

[^1969]: Heirmos of Ode 3 (Canon of St. Athanasius, Tone 8), praising Christ Who established the heavens and the earth (Ps. 101:26 [102:25] LXX; 1 Sam. 2:2 [1 Kgs. 2:2 LXX]).

[^1970]: Troparion of St. Athanasius, extolling his purity of soul and body as a fitting temple of the Holy Trinity.

[^1971]: Troparion praising Athanasius as a vessel cleansed of passions, illuminated by the Holy Spirit as a cosmic luminary.

[^1972]: Troparion hailing Athanasius as the living canon of the episcopate, uniting practical virtue (*praxis*) with contemplative theology (*theoria*), echoing St. Gregory the Theologian's eulogy.

[^1973]: Marian Theotokion honoring the Mother of God as more exalted than the celestial orders for bearing the Uncircumscribed God.

[^1974]: Heirmos of Ode 3 (Canon of St. Cyril, Tone 4), invoking Christ as the Church's strength and rock of refuge (1 Sam. 2:2 [1 Kgs. 2:2 LXX]).

[^1975]: Troparion of St. Cyril, portraying him breaking demonic snares like cobwebs through Christ's power.

[^1976]: Troparion of St. Cyril, casting down heretical haughtiness exalted against the knowledge of Christ (2 Cor. 10:5).

[^1977]: Troparion commending Cyril's ascetic mastery over bodily pleasures.

[^1978]: Marian Theotokion confessing the Virgin pre-purified by the Holy Spirit at the Annunciation (Lk. 1:35) to bear the divine Logos.

[^1979]: Kontakion of SS. Athanasius and Cyril (Tone 4, *Thou hast appeared today*), petitioning the arch-hierarchs to protect the faithful.

[^1980]: Sessional Hymn of St. Athanasius (Tone 3, *Awed by the beauty of thy virginity*), celebrating his predestined victory over Arianism.

[^1981]: Sessional Hymn of St. Cyril (Tone 3), praising his confession of the consubstantial Trinity and unconfused hypostases.

[^1982]: Sessional Marian Theotokion, venerating the incomprehensible mystery of the Incarnation of the Uncircumscribed from the Virgin's pure blood.

[^1983]: Stavrotheotokion at the Sessional Hymns, the Mother of God lamenting Christ's voluntary Crucifixion.

[^1984]: Heirmos of Ode 4 (Canon of St. Athanasius, Tone 8), quoting the Canticle of Habakkuk (Hab. 3:2-3): "Thou art my strength, O Lord..."

[^1985]: Troparion portraying Athanasius inspired by the Pentecostal Spirit (Acts 2:1-4) as a thirteenth Apostle defending Orthodoxy.

[^1986]: Troparion recalling Athanasius' decisive role as archdeacon at the First Ecumenical Council of Nicaea (325 AD) before ascending the throne of Alexandria.

[^1987]: Troparion allegorizing Athanasius as an axe felling heretical weeds and cultivating the fruitful seed of the Gospel.

[^1988]: Troparion praising Athanasius for consuming the opposing extremes of Arian division (tritheism/subordinationism) and Sabellian confusion (modalism).

[^1989]: Marian Theotokion of Ode 4 (Athanasius' Canon), hailing the Virgin as the unassailable haven and intercessor for Christians.

[^1990]: Heirmos of Ode 4 (Canon of St. Cyril, Tone 4), the Church beholding the Sun of Righteousness exalted on the Cross (Hab. 3:11).

[^1991]: Troparion of St. Cyril, describing his elevation to the patriarchal throne of Alexandria upon conquering passions.

[^1992]: Troparion celebrating Cyril as the spiritual son and canonical successor of St. Mark the Evangelist in Alexandria.

[^1993]: Troparion depicting Cyril shepherding Christ's flock with wholesome doctrine as nutritious pasture.

[^1994]: Marian Theotokion of Ode 4 (Cyril's Canon), confessing the Creator of all things born in the flesh from the Virgin.

[^1995]: Heirmos of Ode 5 (Canon of St. Athanasius, Tone 8), supplication for spiritual illumination (Isa. 26:9).

[^1996]: Troparion praising Athanasius' refutation of the idolatrous errors of paganism and early heresies (cf. *Contra Gentes*).

[^1997]: Troparion extolling Athanasius' polemical and dogmatic treatises defending the Nicene faith.

[^1998]: Troparion honoring Athanasius as a teacher of ecumenical doctors and successor of apostolic proclamation.

[^1999]: Marian Theotokion of Ode 5 (Athanasius' Canon), pleading the Virgin's singular maternal boldness (*parrhesia*) before her Son.

[^2000]: Heirmos of Ode 5 (Canon of St. Cyril, Tone 4), hailing Christ the true Light coming into the world (Jn. 1:9; 8:12).

[^2001]: Troparion of St. Cyril, now beholding Christ face to face beyond the riddle of earthly mirrors (1 Cor. 13:12).

[^2002]: Troparion comparing Cyril's eloquence to an overwhelming flood drowning heretical errors.

[^2003]: Troparion extolling Cyril for eradicating both Arian division and Sabellian confusion.

[^2004]: Troparion praising Cyril's patristic treatises as treasures of gold and precious stones bequeathed to the Church.

[^2005]: Marian Theotokion of Ode 5 (Cyril's Canon), confessing the Virgin as an invincible weapon and haven of salvation.

[^2006]: Heirmos of Ode 6 (Canon of St. Athanasius, Tone 8), the prayer of the penitent from the abyss of sins (Jon. 2:2-3).

[^2007]: Troparion commending Athanasius for vigorously refuting Apollinarianism, which denied a human rational soul (*nous*) to Christ (cf. *Contra Apollinarium*).

[^2008]: Troparion praising Athanasius' doctrine as living water sweeter than honey flowing from the fountain of grace (Ps. 18:11 [19:10] LXX).

[^2009]: Troparion presenting Miriam (Ex. 15:20) typologically rejoicing at the beauty of Athanasius' orthodox writings and prophetic defense against sprouting heresies.

[^2010]: Marian Theotokion of Ode 6 (Athanasius' Canon), imploring deliverance from sins through the Virgin.

[^2011]: Heirmos of Ode 6 (Canon of St. Cyril, Tone 4), the Church redeemed by the blood and water from Christ's side (Jn. 19:34).

[^2012]: Troparion of St. Cyril, portrayed as a minister distributing the seraphic coal of divine fire for spiritual purification (Isa. 6:6).

[^2013]: Troparion comparing Cyril's destruction of heresies to Samson vanquishing the Philistines (Judg. 15:15-16).

[^2014]: Troparion commemorating Cyril's presidency at the Council of Ephesus (431 AD) refuting the Christological heresy of Nestorius and defending the title *Theotokos*.

[^2015]: Marian Theotokion of Ode 6 (Cyril's Canon), confessing the paradox of the Infinite God contained in the Virgin's womb.

[^2016]: Kontakion of St. Athanasius (Tone 2, *The shedding of thy blood*), celebrating his planting of orthodox dogmas.

[^2018]: Heirmos of Ode 7 (Canon of St. Athanasius, Tone 8), the Three Holy Youths preserved in the Babylonian furnace (Dan. 3:49-50).

[^2019]: Troparion of St. Athanasius (*Athanasios* = "immortal"), proclaiming the co-eternal and consubstantial Trinity.

[^2020]: Troparion recalling Athanasius' five exiles suffered under Arian emperors (Constantius II, Julian, Valens) for preaching the consubstantial Trinity.

[^2021]: Troparion praising Athanasius' adamantine endurance under trials and magnetic power drawing the faithful to Orthodoxy.

[^2022]: Troparion celebrating Athanasius' dogmatic synthesis of the Trinity in three Hypostases and one Essence (*ousia*).

[^2023]: Conclusion of the Marian Theotokion of Ode 7 (Athanasius' Canon), praising the Virgin as the radiant portal of the divine revelation.

[^2027]: Troparion praising Cyril's extensive commentaries on the Old Testament Prophets inspired by the Holy Spirit.

[^2028]: Troparion formulating Cyril's orthodox Trinitarian confession: immutable, unconfused, and indivisible.

[^2033]: Conclusion of Troparion 3 of Ode 8 (Canon of St. Athanasius), Trinitarian doxology confessing Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.

[^2038]: Troparion setting forth the Cyrilline Christological formula: Christ one incarnate Person (*hypostasis*) in two natures, indivisible and unconfused.

[^2970]: Ps. 111:6 [LXX 111:6 / MT 112:6].

[^2971]: St. Hippolytus of Rome, celebrated on January 30 alongside the Three Hierarchs.

[^2972]: Addressed to St. Basil the Great, the first of the Three Hierarchs in this stichera triad.

[^2973]: Lit., "of the great God-originating ones" (богонача́льныхъ вели́кихъ).

[^2974]: Ps. 18:5 [LXX 18:5 / MT 19:4].

[^2975]: Ps. 131:9 [LXX 131:9 / MT 132:9].

[^2976]: Alluding to the Parable of the Laborers in the Vineyard (Mt. 20:1–16) and the Parable of the Talents (Mt. 25:14–30).

[^2977]: Ps. 18:4 [LXX 18:4 / MT 19:3].

[^2978]: Deut. 1:8–17 [LXX 1:8–17].

[^2979]: Deut. 10:14–16 [LXX 10:14–16], continuing onto the following leaf.

[^2980]: Wis. 3:1–9.

[^2981]: By Nilus Xanthopoulos (14th century hymnographer). The names of the Three Hierarchs are poetically interpreted according to their Greek etymologies: Basil (βασίλειος, "kingly"), Gregory (theologos, "theologian"), and John (Ἰωάννης, from the Hebrew Yochanan, "grace of Yahweh", Slavonic благодать).

[^2982]: *Birthgiver of God*: Church Slavonic *Богородица* (Theotokos).

[^2983]: *John Chrysostom*: Church Slavonic *Іѡаннъ Златоустый*, literally *John of the Golden Mouth*.

[^2984]: *Hieromartyr Hippolytus*: St. Hippolytus, Pope of Rome, commemorated liturgically on January 30 alongside the Three Holy Hierarchs.

[^2985]: *thy virginity*: Church Slavonic *дѣтства* (literally *childhood/maidenhood*, collated with Greek *παρθενίας*, virginity).

[^2986]: *The Word co-unoriginate*: Church Slavonic *Собезнача́льное Сло́во*, model melody of Tone 5 (attested in the text as *Собезначáлное*).

[^2987]: *Gospel according to John, pericope 36*: John 10:9–16, the standard Festal Gospel reading for holy hierarchs ("I am the door... I am the good shepherd").

[^2988]: *John of Euchaita*: Metropolitan John Mauropous of Euchaita (11th century), author of the canonical office to the Three Holy Hierarchs.

[^2989]: *Acrostic*: The Church Slavonic acrostic reads: *Трїсо́лнечнагѡ свѣ́та, трѝ возвыша́ю сїѧ̑нца* ("I praise the three rays of the Tri-sunned light"), composed in Byzantine iambic verse by John Mauropous.

[^2990]: *Katavasia*: Ode 1 Katavasia of the Meeting of the Lord (Church Slavonic *Сꙋ́шꙋ глубороди́тельнꙋю зе́млю*, "The sun once traversed the dry depth-bearing land").

[^2991]: *Katavasia*: Ode 3 Katavasia of the Meeting of the Lord (Church Slavonic *Оу҆твержде́ніе на тѧ̀ надѣ́ющихсѧ*, "O Lord, the confirmation of them that put their trust in Thee, establish the Church...").

[^2992]: *morning rain that came at the end*: Scriptural allusion to Hosea 6:3, Joel 2:23, and James 5:7 concerning the early and latter rain.

[^2993]: *twofold according to nature, of two wills and twofold operation*: The dogmatic definition of the Sixth Ecumenical Council (Constantinople III, 680–681) against Monothelitism and Monoenergism, affirming two natural wills and two natural operations in Jesus Christ.

[^2994]: *Katavasia*: Ode 5 Katavasia of the Meeting of the Lord (Habakkuk 3:3; Church Slavonic *Покры́ла є҆́сть небеса̀ добродѣ́тель твоѧ̀, Хрїсте́*).

[^2995]: *Katavasia*: Ode 6 Katavasia of the Meeting of the Lord (Isaiah 6:1; Church Slavonic *Ꙗ҆́кѡ ви́дѣ И҆са́їа ѻ҆́бразнѡ*).

[^2996]: *the unbegottenness of the Father, the begetting of the Word, and the procession of the Spirit Himself*: The classical Cappadocian hypostatic properties (*ἰδιώματα*) formulated by St. Basil the Great and St. Gregory the Theologian distinguishing the persons of the Holy Trinity.

[^2997]: *cause of all created things*: Church Slavonic *винꙋ̀ тли́нъ* (an epigraphic rendering reflecting Greek *αἰτίαν κτισμάτων* / *τῶν γενητῶν*).

[^2998]: *Katavasia*: Ode 6 Katavasia of the Meeting of the Lord (Jonah 2:3; Church Slavonic *Возопѝ къ тебѣ̀ ви́дѣвъ ста́рецъ*).

[^2999]: *Katavasia*: Ode 7 Katavasia of the Meeting of the Lord (Church Slavonic *Тебѐ во ѻ҆гнѝ ѡ҆роси́вшаго ѻ҆́троки бг҃осло́вившыѧ*).

[^3000]: *Katavasia*: Ode 8 Katavasia of the Meeting of the Lord (Church Slavonic *Нестерпи́момꙋ ѻ҆гню̀ соедини́вшеся, бг҃оче́стїѧ предстоѧ́ще ю҆́ношы*).

[^3001]: *the three shepherds, the defenders*: Church Slavonic *приты́лцы* (literally *those standing at the back/defenders*, or *fellow-protectors*).

[^3002]: *Katavasia*: Ode 9 Katavasia of the Meeting of the Lord (Hebrews 10:1; Church Slavonic *Въ зако́нѣ сѣ́ни и҆ писа́ній ѻ҆́бразъ ви́димъ вѣ́рнїи*).

[^3003]: *O unwaning Light*: Church Slavonic model melody *Свѣ́те неизмѣ́нный* (Tone 3).

[^3004]: *the God-bearing fathers*: The physical printed edition reads *є҆гоно́сныѧ*, an epigraphic misprint for *бг҃оно́сныѧ* (God-bearing).

[^3005]: *He Who adorned the heaven with stars*: Church Slavonic model melody *И҆́же не́бо ѕвѣзда́ми* (Tone 3).

[^3006]: *Neil the Chalcidican*: Church Slavonic *Ні́ла халкопꙋ́ла* (Nilus Chalcopolus or Neil the Metropolitan of Rhodes / John Neil Mauropous of Euchaita).

[^3007]: *Germanus the Patriarch*: St. Germanus I, Patriarch of Constantinople (715–730).

[^3008]: *Hebrews, pericope 334*: Hebrews 13:7–16 ("Remember your leaders, who have spoken unto you the word of God... Jesus Christ is the same yesterday and today and unto ages").

[^3009]: *The heavens shall confess...*: LXX Psalm 88:6 ("The heavens shall confess Thy wonders, O Lord, and Thy truth in the congregation of the saints").

[^3010]: *Communion Verse: Rejoice in the Lord*: LXX Psalm 32:1 ("Rejoice in the Lord, O ye righteous; praise becomes the upright", Slavonic *Ра́дꙋйтеся пра́веднїи ѡ҆ Го́сподѣ*).

[^3011]: *Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee*: The opening pre-Lenten Sunday of the Lenten Triodion cycle.

[^3012]: *Sunday of the Prodigal Son*: The second pre-Lenten preparatory Sunday.

[^3013]: *Meatfare Sunday*: The third pre-Lenten preparatory Sunday, dedicated to the Commemoration of the Second Coming of Christ and the Universal Last Judgment.

[^3014]: *By the rivers of Babylon*: LXX Psalm 136, the third Polyeleos psalm prescribed alongside Psalms 134 and 135 on the pre-Lenten Sundays of the Prodigal Son, Meatfare, and Cheesefare.

[^3015]: *The sun once shone upon the dry land*: Heirmos of Ode 1 of the Canon of the Meeting of the Lord (Church Slavonic *Сꙋ́шꙋ глꙋбороди́тельнꙋю зе́млю*).

[^3016]: *Meatfare Saturday*: The Saturday before Meatfare Sunday, dedicated to the Universal Commemoration of all the Faithful Departed (Soul Saturday). Because of the solemn requiem character of this day, the festive vigil of the Three Hierarchs is transferred forward to the preceding Friday.

[^3017]: *Cheesefare Week*: The week immediately preceding Great Lent (Maslenitsa), during which meat is forbidden but dairy products and eggs are permitted throughout the week.

[^3018]: *Where there is a triode*: Church Slavonic *И҆ди́же трїпѣ́снѡ*, referring to the three-ode canons appointed in the Lenten Triodion for weekdays of Cheesefare Week.

[^3019]: *Cyrus and John*: The Holy Unmercenary Wonderworkers Cyrus (an Egyptian physician from Alexandria who became an anchorite in Arabia) and John (a former Roman soldier from Edessa), who together encouraged the Christian woman Athanasia and her three young daughters (Theodotia, Theoktiste, and Eudoxia) and were beheaded under Diocletian at Canopus in 311.

[^3020]: *Joy of the heavenly ranks*: Church Slavonic model melody *Небе́сныхъ чинѡ́въ* (Tone 1).

[^3021]: *O most marvelous wonder*: Church Slavonic model melody *Ѽ пресла́внагѡ чꙋдесѐ* (Tone 8).

[^3022]: *When from the Tree*: Church Slavonic model melody *Е҆гда̀ ѿ дре́ва* (Tone 2).

[^3023]: *manfully vanquished*: The physical print shows faint inking or a paper crease between *мꙋ́жески* and *ди́ша*, reading *[побѣ]ди́ша* ("manfully conquered/vanquished").

[^3024]: *Thou Who wast lifted up*: Church Slavonic model melody *Вознесы́йся* (Tone 4).

[^3025]: *good torments*: Church Slavonic *бл҃гое мꙋ́къ*, an epigraphic reading reflecting *[бо]я́знь мꙋ́къ* ("fear of torments") or Greek *δεινὰ τῶν κολάσεων* ("terrors of torments").

[^3026]: *arrows of babes*: Scriptural citation from LXX Psalm 63:8 ("The arrows of babes have become their wounds").

[^3027]: *Today the Virgin*: Church Slavonic model melody *Дѣ́ва дне́сь* (Tone 3).

[^3028]: *He Who adorned the heaven with stars*: Church Slavonic model melody *И҆́же не́бо ѕвѣзда́ми* (Tone 3).

[^3029]: *Theodotia, Eudoxia, and Theoktiste*: The three young daughters of St. Athanasia (aged fifteen, thirteen, and eleven respectively), martyred together with their mother and the holy unmercenaries Cyrus and John at Canopus near Alexandria in 311.

[^3030]: *strangers*: Church Slavonic *приходцы*, meaning pilgrims, sojourners, or newcomers (*προσήλυτοι* / *ἐπήλυδες*).

[^3031]: *Called from on high*: Church Slavonic model melody *Зва́нный свы́ше* (Tone 4).

[^3032]: *1 Corinthians, pericope 153*: 1 Corinthians 12:27–13:8 ("Now ye are the body of Christ, and members in particular...").

[^3033]: *Matthew, pericope 34 from the half*: Matthew 10:1, 5–8 ("And when He had called unto Him His twelve disciples, He gave them power against unclean spirits, to cast them out, and to heal all manner of sickness and all manner of disease...").

[^3034]: *Communion Verse*: LXX Psalm 32:1 ("Rejoice in the Lord, O ye righteous").

[^3035]: *Typographic Tailpiece*: A characteristic Pochaiv monastic woodcut printer's mark bearing the radiate IHS monogram with cross and three nails, standardly employed by the Basilian Fathers of the Holy Dormition Pochaiv Lavra in their liturgical imprints.

[^3036]: *Catchword: Theotokia*: The catchword *Бг҃оро́дичны* at the lower margin indicates the sequence of Dismissal Theotokia for the eight tones found appended to the conclusion of the monthly volumes in the full liturgical set.

[^3037]: *Concluding Folio 224v*: This leaf concludes the text of the Month of January (leaves p1 through p450, folios 1r through 224v), completing the facsimiles and transcription of Volume 5 of the 1761 Pochaiv Menaion.
